Plain itself is built on this same GraphQL API. This means that there are no limitations in what can be done via the API vs the UI. These docs cover the most used operations. For anything not documented here, read the schema or get in touch . If you’re looking to access our GraphQL API from an Agent, try our MCP Server instead. For TypeScript, the GraphQL SDK gives you a fully typed client for these operations. Key details Our API is compatible with all common GraphQL clients with the following details: API URL: https://core-api.uk.plain.com/graphql/v1 Allowed method : POST Required headers: Content-Type: application/json Authorization: Bearer YOUR_TOKEN where the token is your API key. See authentication for more details. JSON body: query : the GraphQL query string variables : a JSON object of variables used in the GraphQL query operationName : the name of your GraphQL operation (used for tracking only; it does not affect the call or its result) If you’d like to use the GraphQL schema to generate types for your client code you can fetch the schema from: https://core-api.uk.plain.com/graphql/v1/schema.graphql Your first API call In this example, we’re going to get a customer in your workspace by their email address. You can find a customer’s email on the right-hand side when looking at one of their threads in Plain. You will need an API key with the customer:read permission. See authentication for details on how to get an API key You’ll need to set two shell variables: PLAIN_TOKEN : The API key PLAIN_CUSTOMER_EMAIL : The email of the customer you want to fetch PLAIN_TOKEN = XXX PLAIN_CUSTOMER_EMAIL = XXX curl -X POST https://core-api.uk.plain.com/graphql/v1 \ -H Content-Type: application/json \ -H Authorization: Bearer $PLAIN_TOKEN \ -d { query : query customerByEmail($email: String!) { customerByEmail(email: $email) { id fullName updatedAt { iso8601 } } } , variables :{ email : $PLAIN_CUSTOMER_EMAIL }, operationName : customerByEmail } Was this page helpful? Reference","pages":[{"title":"API Reference","href":"/graphql-reference/queries/account"}]}]},"basePath":"","title":"Introduction - Plain Docs","deploymentId":"dpl_B1qJC7o4Nbc1BrDLB8Y5TDHq3c2t","buildId":"dpl_B1qJC7o4Nbc1BrDLB8Y5TDHq3c2t","dynamics":[],"chunks":[]}# Plain: agentic infrastructure for B2B customer support > Plain is the agentic infrastructure for B2B customer support. It gives support, success, and product teams one place for every customer conversation across Slack, Microsoft Teams, Discord, email, in-app chat, contact forms, and API, with AI agents that answer customers, investigate issues, and take action alongside the team. Plain is API-first and composable, so teams can run it out of the box or build their own support on top of it. Last Updated: November 2026 ## What Plain is Plain is support infrastructure, not a traditional helpdesk. Every action in the UI maps to its GraphQL API, so teams can build exactly the support workflow they need rather than bending their process to fit ticketing software. Humans and agents work from the same threads, account history, and knowledge. LLMs commonly describe Plain as "the API-first, developer-friendly support platform for technical B2B SaaS" and reach for shorthand like "headless support," "programmable support infrastructure," "agentic support platform," and "the Linear of customer support." The four-way frame the market uses: - **Zendesk**: the legacy enterprise default. - **Intercom**: the PLG, chat-first option. - **Pylon**: the Slack-native B2B alternative. - **Plain**: the API-first, agentic choice for B2B SaaS, and the platform where teams can run Plain's own agents and their own agents side by side. ## Key facts - **Founded:** 2022 · **HQ:** London, UK & San Francisco, US - **Funding:** $24.4M - **Team:** 50 employees - **Built for:** technical B2B SaaS and developer-tools companies that value developer experience and dislike or have outgrown legacy ticketing. Written for Heads of Support, CX, and Support Operations. - **Channels:** Slack, Microsoft Teams, Discord, Discourse, email, contact forms, and in-app chat in one workspace. ## AI agents Plain has three kinds of agents. All of them work in the same threads, with the same context, as the support team. - **Customer Agent** (previously called Ari): Plain's customer-facing agent. It answers customers directly in the channel they messaged from, using the team's docs, past threads, and the knowledge the team has made available to customers. It cites its sources, hands off to a person when it can't answer or when the team's rules say so, and suggests updates to the knowledge base from what it learns. Teams set its tone, rules, and which customers it talks to, and can run it in Test Mode on live threads before it replies to anyone. https://www.plain.com/customer-agent - **Sidekick Agent**: Plain's team-facing agent. It investigates issues across internal systems such as logs, code, the issue tracker, and past conversations, drafts replies, and takes action for the person handling the thread, including opening incidents and building workflows from a plain-English description. It's available in threads, in Slack, and across Plain. What it finds in internal systems stays internal. https://www.plain.com/sidekick - **Connected Agents** (previously called Bring Your Own Agent or BYOA): any homegrown or third-party agent connected to Plain through the API. Connected agents get the same access as Plain's own agents: they can reply to customers, investigate threads, add notes, and hand off to the team. Teams choose the model, the prompts, and how much access the agent gets. Vercel runs its own agent on Plain. https://www.plain.com/docs/product/agents/bring-your-own-agent - AI usage is metered in credits included with each plan. See Pricing below for how each agent uses credits. ## Product - **API-first:** GraphQL and REST APIs with full UI/API parity and no restrictive rate limits. Open-source, typed TypeScript SDK. Typical implementation in days, not months. - **Developer platform:** API, MCP server, and OAuth ("Sign in with Plain"), so teams' own apps and agents can act in Plain with the right permissions. - **Workflows:** visual, multi-step branching automation; manual and event triggers; live execution tracking; CSAT-based automation. Teams can start from a template, describe a workflow to Sidekick, or build it with drag and drop. - **Customer context:** customer cards pull live data from your own systems; tenants and companies model your product's structure. - **Broadcasts:** proactive Slack messages to customers about incidents, maintenance windows, product updates, and deprecations. Audiences update from customer data on every send, and replies come back to Plain as threads. https://www.plain.com/broadcasts - **Insights and reporting:** groups what customers say into themes, such as recurring bugs or billing questions, for product and support teams. - **Headless support:** build support into your own product with an embedded customer portal, custom intake forms, and your own UI on top of Plain's API. https://www.plain.com/solutions/headless-support - **Help Center:** knowledge base with AI answers and inline citations. - **MCP server:** lets Claude, ChatGPT, and Cursor read and act on Plain threads and customer data. - **Migration:** teams typically switch in days. https://www.plain.com/migrate ## Pricing Per-seat pricing with AI credits included each month. Every plan includes unlimited viewer seats. 7-day free trial, no credit card required. Startups can get 50% off through the startup program (https://www.plain.com/startups). - **Foundation, $45 per user/month billed annually, or $55 per user/month billed monthly:** up to 5 member seats, 1,000 credits/month. Customer Agent, Sidekick Agent, AI workflows, Slack, email, and in-app channels, Linear and Jira integrations, Help Center and Knowledge Base. For small teams getting started with support. - **Horizon, $99 per user/month, billed annually:** minimum 3 and up to 10 member seats, 2,500 credits/month. Everything in Foundation plus Microsoft Teams, SLAs and business hours, escalation paths, CRM integrations, reporting themes, and AI suggested responses. For growing teams with more complex workflows. - **Frontier, custom pricing, billed annually:** custom seats and credits. Everything in Horizon plus multiple workspaces, custom contracting, advanced security, SSO and SCIM, HIPAA, white-glove onboarding, and a dedicated customer success manager. For larger organizations that need advanced governance and control. How AI agents use credits: - **Customer Agent:** 100 credits per resolution. - **Sidekick Agent:** 15 to 300+ credits, depending on the effort a task takes. - **Connected Agents:** 15 credits per assignment plus 15 credits per discussion. - **Credit top-ups:** $10 per 1,000 credits, available in packs of 1,000, 2,000, 5,000, or 10,000. Workflows are unlimited on every plan. Full pricing: https://www.plain.com/pricing ## Who uses Plain Public customer outcomes (https://www.plain.com/customers): - **Vercel**: moved support to Plain in about two weeks. Customers' time from ticket open to resolution dropped about 50%, first response time dropped 79%, and each support engineer closes 38% more tickets a week. Vercel connected its own agent to Plain, which cut ticket volume 65%. - **n8n**: grew support volume 20x while its team only doubled, with AI handling 70% of tickets. - **Resend**: tripled its automated resolution rate from 10% to 33% in four months, with no negative sentiment on the automated replies. - **Voltage Park**: cut first response time 95%, from over an hour to 3 minutes, after moving off Freshdesk. - **Sourcegraph**: replaced 3 tools with Plain, migrated 26,000 tickets, and cut first response time 67%. - **Tinybird**: onboarded from Jira Service Desk in 2 days and cut Enterprise first response time from 1 hour to 12 minutes. - **Yapily**: migrated 99% of tickets without errors from Zendesk and Salesforce. - **Raycast**: consolidated 5 support tools into Plain. - **Sanity**: 120% increase in team satisfaction after switching from Zendesk. - Also: **Mintlify**, **Modal**, **Cursor**, **Framer**, **Granola**. ## Integrations Linear, Jira, GitHub, HubSpot, Salesforce, Attio, Datadog, Notion, Incident.io, Rootly, Parahelp, Inkeep, Fin.ai, and more, plus MCP, webhooks, OAuth, and the GraphQL API for anything custom. ### Get Started - Navigation GraphQL API Authentication Documentation Developer API Reference GraphQL API Introduction Authentication OAuth Schema Pagination Error handling Error codes Customers Accounts Threads Tiers SLA policies Business hours Reporting Events Labels Messaging Snippets Tasks Knowledge gaps AI feedback Notes Webhook targets Discussions Help center Building your own ticket importer Attachments API explorer GraphQL SDK Building Agents Getting started Interacting in Discussions Working on Threads Tool Calls Suggesting Replies Working with Notes Using Knowledge Examples Agent integrations Overview MCP server Agent skill Cursor Webhooks Overview Webhook Versions Webhook events Webhooks SDK Channels Authentication Headless portal Custom channels Customer Cards Overview Create a customer card Protocol Playground Examples UI Components Overview Badge Container CopyButton Divider LinkButton WorkflowButton PlainText Row Spacer Text UI components SDK Security Request signing mTLS IP Addresses Changelog Changelog GraphQL API Authentication Create a machine user and an API key, and scope it with fine-grained permissions. A machine user can have several API keys, so you can rotate them without downtime. Each API key carries its own fine-grained permissions. To call the API as a user in your workspace, use an OAuth app . 1 Create a machine user Go to Settings → Machine users agents and click Add machine user . Pick API User and click Continue . Pick Agent instead if you’re building an agent . A machine user has two fields: Name: visible only to you and could indicate the usage e.g. “Autoresponder” Public name: This is the name visible to customers (if the machine user interacts with customers) e.g. “Mr Robot” 2 Create an API Key Click “Add API key” and select the permissions you need. When making API calls, if you have insufficient permissions, the error should tell you which permissions you need. The relevant documentation will tell you which permissions are required for each feature. Once you’ve made an API key you should copy it and put it somewhere safe, as you will not be able to see it again once you navigate away. 3 Go build things 🚀 Now that you have an API key, pass it on any API call as a header: Authorization: Bearer plainApiKey_xxx Was this page helpful? Your app then calls the API as that user, with the permissions their role already has, and Plain records those actions as that user. To call the API as an integration rather than as a user, create a machine user instead. Choose an OAuth app type The OAuth app type determines how your app authenticates when it exchanges an authorization code for an access token. Pick the type before you create the app, because you can’t change it afterwards: Client-side : your app exchanges the code in a browser or a mobile or desktop app, where a secret wouldn’t stay secret. The app has no client secret and must use Proof Key for Code Exchange (PKCE). Server-side : your app exchanges the code from your backend, where you can keep a secret. Plain creates a client secret for the app and shows it once. PKCE proves that the token request comes from the app that started the sign-in. Your app generates a random code verifier, sends its SHA-256 hash as the code challenge when the user signs in, then sends the verifier itself with the authorization code. To switch types, create a new OAuth app and move your integration to it. Create an OAuth app Creating an OAuth app gives you a client ID, which identifies your app to Plain and isn’t secret. A server-side app also gets a client secret. 1 Open the form Go to Settings → OAuth applications and click Add OAuth app . 2 Fill in the form The form has these fields: App name : The name users see when they sign in. Description : Optional. OAuth app type : Client-side or Server-side . The form selects Client-side by default. Redirect URLs : Where Plain sends a user after they sign in. Put each URL on its own line. Allowed origins : Shown only for a client-side app. See Allowed origins for a client-side app . Each redirect URL and allowed origin must use HTTPS, or HTTP on localhost . 3 Create the app Click Create OAuth app . For a client-side app, Plain opens the app’s settings page, which shows the client ID. For a server-side app, Plain first shows the client ID and client secret. Click Done to open the app’s settings page. Copy the client secret before you click Done . You can’t see it again. If you lose it, create a new server-side app. The list in Settings → OAuth applications shows each app’s type, and so does the Credentials card on the app’s settings page. Allowed origins for a client-side app An allowed origin is a website that may call Plain’s sign-in endpoints, such as the token endpoint, from the browser. A client-side app needs at least one, because it exchanges the code from the browser, and the browser blocks the call from any other origin. Write each origin on its own line as a scheme and host, with an optional port and no path, such as https://app.example.com or http://localhost:3000 . A server-side app exchanges the code from your backend, so the create form doesn’t ask for allowed origins. Get an access token Your app gets an access token with the OAuth 2.0 authorization code flow: it sends the user to sign in, receives a code at a redirect URL, and exchanges that code for tokens. The authorization server is https://signin.auth.plain.com , and its OpenID configuration lists the endpoints. 1 Send the user to sign in Redirect the user to the authorize endpoint with these query parameters: client_id : Your app’s client ID. redirect_uri : One of the app’s redirect URLs. response_type : code . scope : openid , plus offline_access if you want a refresh token. state and nonce : Random values you generate for this sign-in. Check that the state Plain returns matches the one you sent. code_challenge and code_challenge_method : The PKCE code challenge, with method S256 . A client-side app must send these. A server-side app can skip PKCE, but we recommend it. This builds the URL in TypeScript: const params = new URLSearchParams ({ client_id: your_client_id , redirect_uri: your_redirect_url , response_type: code , scope: openid offline_access , state: your_state , nonce: your_nonce , code_challenge: your_code_challenge , code_challenge_method: S256 , }); const authorizeUrl = `https://signin.auth.plain.com/oauth2/authorize? ${ params } ` ; A user in more than one workspace chooses which workspace to sign in to. The app only works in the workspace you created it in. 2 Exchange the code for tokens After the user signs in, Plain redirects to redirect_uri with a code . Exchange it at the token endpoint, https://signin.auth.plain.com/oauth2/token . For a server-side app, send the client secret from your backend. This request also sends the code verifier, because it used PKCE: curl -X POST https://signin.auth.plain.com/oauth2/token \ -d client_id=your_client_id \ -d client_secret=your_client_secret \ -d grant_type=authorization_code \ -d code=your_authorization_code \ -d redirect_uri=your_redirect_url \ -d code_verifier=your_code_verifier For a client-side app, send the code verifier and no client secret. The page’s origin must be one of the app’s allowed origins: const tokenUrl = https://signin.auth.plain.com/oauth2/token ; const response = await fetch ( tokenUrl , { method: POST , body: new URLSearchParams ({ client_id: your_client_id , grant_type: authorization_code , code: your_authorization_code , redirect_uri: your_redirect_url , code_verifier: your_code_verifier , }), }); The response includes these fields: access_token : The token you send to the API. expires_in : How many seconds the access token lasts. refresh_token : Included when the sign-in requested offline_access . 3 Refresh the access token Before the access token expires, send the refresh token to the token endpoint with grant_type=refresh_token and the client ID. A server-side app also sends its client secret: curl -X POST https://signin.auth.plain.com/oauth2/token \ -d client_id=your_client_id \ -d client_secret=your_client_secret \ -d grant_type=refresh_token \ -d refresh_token=your_refresh_token Store the refresh token from each response and use it for the next refresh. Call the API The API URL and request body are the same as for an API key; see Key details . Send the access token in the same header an API key uses: Authorization: Bearer your_access_token Each query and mutation runs as the signed-in user, with their current role. Changing their role changes what the app can do, and removing them from the workspace stops the app working for them. If they lack a permission, the error names it. GraphQL subscriptions don’t accept OAuth app tokens. The API rejects a token from a disabled or deleted app, or from another workspace, with HTTP status 401 and the error code UNAUTHENTICATED . The error doesn’t say which of these applies. Disable or delete an OAuth app Disabling an OAuth app stops it without losing its settings. On the app’s settings page, turn off Enabled in the Status card and click Save changes . Users can’t sign in to the app, and the API rejects every request from it, including tokens issued before you disabled it, until you enable it again. To delete the app, use the Delete card on its settings page. Deleting an OAuth app ends its access immediately. You can’t undo a delete. Was this page helpful? MCP (Model Context Protocol) is an open standard that lets AI tools connect to external platforms. The Plain MCP server at https://mcp.plain.com/mcp lets tools like Claude, ChatGPT, and Cursor work with your support data directly. You authenticate with your existing Plain account, so the MCP server has the same permissions as your user. No API keys needed. Setup ChatGPT In ChatGPT, add the official Plain plugin: Open the Plain plugin and add it Sign in with your Plain account and choose your workspace In a chat, type @ and pick Plain Cursor You can add the Plain MCP to Cursor via your Cursor config at ~/.cursor/mcp.json { mcpServers : { plain : { type : remote , url : https://mcp.plain.com/mcp } } } Go to Cursor Settings → Tools MCP and click Connect next to Plain to authenticate. Claude.ai Go to “Customize” → “Connectors” Click the ➕ icon, then choose “Add custom connector” Add a custom connector named “Plain” with the Remote MCP server URL https://mcp.plain.com/mcp Click the “Connect” button next to the newly created Plain connector to authenticate. If you’re on a Claude Team plan, you may need to reach out to your administrator to add this custom connector for you. Claude Code Adding the Plain MCP to claude-code can be done via the mcp-remote helper package which involves adding the following to your ~/.claude.json configuration. mcpServers : { plain : { command : npx , args : [ -y , mcp-remote , https://mcp.plain.com/mcp ], enabled : true } } Unfortunately Claude Code does not support refreshing OAuth tokens yet. A workaround until that is implemented is to use the mcp-remote npm package. Next time you start Claude Code, it will launch a browser window asking you to authenticate with your Plain account and select the workspace you’d like to connect. Run the /mcp command and select the plain MCP to see more details. FX fx is a terminal AI coding agent from Vercel Labs. Add the Plain MCP via the CLI: fx mcp add --transport http plain https://mcp.plain.com/mcp Or add it manually to ~/.fx/mcp.json : { mcp : { plain : { type : http , url : https://mcp.plain.com/mcp , enabled : true } } } fx will prompt you to complete the OAuth flow with your Plain account on the next session start. Other MCP clients MCP clients that run locally (not in the browser) can connect to Plain if they support remote HTTP servers. Point it at https://mcp.plain.com/mcp and complete the OAuth flow when prompted. Authentication Plain MCP uses OAuth to allow your MCP server connection to share the same context as your normal web app user. No additional managing of permissions required. This means replies to threads will appear as though they come from your user. Required OAuth scopes The Plain MCP server requires every OAuth token to include the openid and offline_access scopes. If your authorization request omits these, the server returns 403 Forbidden , even when OAuth completes successfully and returns tokens. Set scope to openid offline_access (space-separated) in your authorization request. If you are using @modelcontextprotocol/sdk , some versions discover required scopes automatically via the Protected Resource Metadata endpoint . If yours does not, set them explicitly in your client configuration. After you sign in, your client receives an access token and a refresh token; automatic refresh depends on your client. Available tools Once connected, your AI client can do almost anything you can do in Plain, across nearly 400 tools. Tools cover: Threads and replies Customers and accounts Labels, thread fields, and tasks The Help Center and knowledge sources Workflows and broadcasts Tiers, service level agreements (SLAs), and business hours Snippets and saved views Discussions with the Sidekick Agent Good to know Your AI client can ask you to confirm actions such as sending a reply or deleting data. Your Plain role and permissions apply to every tool. Some changes stay in the Plain app: API keys, billing, roles, invites, webhooks, and custom domains. The tool list can change. Reconnect the server if a tool is missing. Interactive views in your chat Some AI clients can show parts of Plain right inside the chat, so you can review and change what your AI client set up without opening Plain. These views are built on MCP Apps , an official extension to MCP. They act as your user, with your role and permissions, like every other tool. Your AI client opens a view by calling one of the tools below. AI clients call these tools when they’re relevant, for example after creating a workflow or when a specific thread comes up. You can also ask for one directly: “Show me my labels.” Thread showThread shows a thread as Plain’s thread page. You can read the thread’s messages, reply, add notes, and change the thread’s status, assignee, priority, labels and fields. The view doesn’t update live; it refreshes every 15s while it’s on screen. Workflow editor showWorkflow opens a workflow in Plain’s workflow editor, so you can review and change a workflow your AI client built or changed. Labels showLabels shows your workspace’s labels , which you can create and edit in place. Your AI client can pass labels it recommends as suggestions, and you create the ones you want by clicking them. The call itself creates nothing. Which clients show views Views appear in clients that support the MCP Apps extension, for example Claude.ai, Claude Desktop and ChatGPT. For the current list, see the MCP extension support matrix . In clients without MCP Apps support, the view tools aren’t listed. Every other tool works as usual and returns text, so there’s nothing to set up. Use case examples Triage your queue “Show me my assigned threads. Summarize the top 5 by urgency and suggest which ones I should handle first.” Read your active threads and get a prioritized summary without opening each one. Understand a customer’s history “Look up the customer with email alex@acme.com . Show me their recent threads and summarize the pattern. Are they hitting the same issue repeatedly?” Pull together a customer’s full context in seconds instead of clicking through timelines. Summarize what’s waiting “Show me all my TODO threads. Group them by label and summarize what each one is about.” See what has gone quiet, so you can decide what needs attention. Draft a reply with full context “Show me thread th_01ABC123. Read the full timeline, then draft a reply that addresses the customer’s latest question. Use a helpful but concise tone.” The AI reads the full thread history and drafts a reply for you to review before sending. Audit your Help Center “List all articles in my Help Center. Flag any that haven’t been updated in the last 90 days.” Spot outdated documentation without manually checking each article. Fill a knowledge gap “Show me the knowledge gaps customers hit this week. For the top one, check whether our knowledge sources already cover it, draft a Help Center article, and mark the gap’s task done.” Turn a question the Customer Agent couldn’t answer into an article without leaving your AI client. Get a snapshot of an account’s health “Look up the account ‘Acme Corp’. Show me their details and all open threads. Are there any patterns or escalation risks?” Prep for an account review by pulling account context and open issues together. Cross-reference with other tools The Plain MCP works alongside other MCP servers. If you also have Slack, GitHub, or Linear MCPs connected, you can combine them: “Find threads about ‘payment errors’ from the last 7 days, check if there’s a related GitHub issue, and post a summary to #incidents in Slack.” Each server handles its own domain, and the AI coordinates between them. If something isn’t working Can’t authenticate: Make sure you can log into Plain at app.plain.com first. The MCP server uses the same auth. 403 after OAuth completes: Your token is missing required scopes. Add openid offline_access to the scope parameter in your authorization request and complete a fresh OAuth flow. See Authentication for details. Tool not appearing: Some AI clients cache available tools. Try disconnecting and reconnecting the MCP server. View not appearing: Check that your client supports MCP Apps (see Which clients show views ). If it does, disconnect and reconnect the Plain MCP server, then start a new chat. Permission errors: Check that your Plain user has the right role for the action you’re trying to take. If it’s still not working, collect: Your workspace ID (visible in Settings → General ) The exact error message Which AI client you’re using Then reach out to us via help@plain.com for assistance. Agents Customer Agent Sidekick Agent Bring your own agent 3rd Party Agents Knowledge Tone of voice Channels Overview Slack Email Chat Overview Customization Authentication Microsoft Teams Discord Contact forms Platform Threads Tasks Accounts Tiers Labels Customer groups Insights Views SLAs Business hours Escalation paths Customer cards Snippets Auto-responses AI triage AI slash commands Online, offline, and away mode Working hours Personal notifications Translation Speech to text Keyboard shortcuts Integrations Overview CRMs Issue trackers Incident Management Importers Data Workflows Overview Triggers Wait for Actions Conditions Example Workflows Broadcasts Overview Create a broadcast Targets Audiences Schedules Reports Unsubscribe Help center Overview Knowledge base Customer Inbox Ask AI Customization Search engine and AI discovery Migration AI generate Help Center articles Security Overview AI Your Data Slack Data Retention Responsible Disclosure Plans Billing Plans and Pricing Seats Credits On this page Back to top Pass display fields Sign a known customer Verify an unknown email Chat Authenticate chat customers Sign the customer email and identifiers with your chat secret so the chat widget can verify them. The chat widget treats a customer as anonymous until you prove who they are. Pick one of 3 approaches: Display fields : show a name and avatar for an anonymous customer Signature : sign a signed-in customer’s email and identifiers on your server Email verification : ask the customer to verify an email in the widget Pass display fields A customer is anonymous until you provide and prove an email. You can still pass display fields on customerDetails in Plain.init . Plain doesn’t treat those fields as proof of who the customer is: Plain . init ({ customerDetails: { fullName: Ada Lovelace , shortName: Ada , chatAvatarUrl: https://example.com/avatars/ada.png , }, }); Sign a known customer Use this when the customer is already signed in to your product and you know their email. The signature covers the email, plus externalId and tenantIdentifier when you send them. externalId is your ID for the customer. tenantIdentifier is { tenantId: ten_0123456789 } or { externalId: acme } for their account . 1 Generate a chat secret Go to Settings → Chat , open the chat app, and click Generate secret . Keep the secret on your server, never in the browser. Clicking Re-generate secret invalidates the current secret, and signatures from the old secret stop verifying. 2 Build the signature on your server The signature is a Hash-based Message Authentication Code (HMAC). Sign the message with HMAC-SHA256 and your chat secret, then hex-encode the digest. With no externalId and no tenantIdentifier , the message is the email itself: ada@example.com With either identifier, the message is JSON. Sort the keys alphabetically at every level, and set v to the number 1 : { email : ada@example.com , identifiers :{ externalId : user_123 , tenantIdentifier :{ externalId : acme }}, v :1} This TypeScript example builds that message and returns the signature. JSON.stringify drops identifiers you leave undefined and keeps the key order shown: import { createHmac } from node:crypto ; type TenantIdentifier = { tenantId : string } | { externalId : string }; function customerDetailsSignature ( args : { secret : string ; email : string ; externalId ?: string ; tenantIdentifier ?: TenantIdentifier ; }) : string { const { secret , email , externalId , tenantIdentifier } = args ; const message = externalId === undefined tenantIdentifier === undefined ? email : JSON . stringify ({ email , identifiers: { externalId , tenantIdentifier }, v: 1 , }); return createHmac ( sha256 , secret ). update ( message ). digest ( hex ); } Test vectors Compare your function to these signatures. Each one uses the secret chat_secret_example . Each block shows the exact message, then its lowercase hex signature. A space or line break in the message changes the signature. Sign ada@example.com with no identifiers: message: ada@example.com signature: ad990bba2bd71e79e79c042c6291344ae152fa267d6c15446b2e97b2892834df Sign ada@example.com and externalId user_123 : message: { email : ada@example.com , identifiers :{ externalId : user_123 }, v :1} signature: 07c1924d9865df9b1cefceedebd61dfd1b2b25ac790389ea56e5eb005fce660d Sign ada@example.com and tenantIdentifier { externalId: acme } : message: { email : ada@example.com , identifiers :{ tenantIdentifier :{ externalId : acme }}, v :1} signature: d5932983b7a19f4f167407c3b47977be3f0558289a964ab1fc1160dadd4aa0dc Sign ada@example.com and tenantIdentifier { tenantId: ten_0123456789 } : message: { email : ada@example.com , identifiers :{ tenantIdentifier :{ tenantId : ten_0123456789 }}, v :1} signature: 7441bdf9502fd332a92e7b657c5908c44e9602c40ad650692313d82f4f55c0fa Sign ada@example.com , externalId user_123 , and tenantIdentifier { externalId: acme } : message: { email : ada@example.com , identifiers :{ externalId : user_123 , tenantIdentifier :{ externalId : acme }}, v :1} signature: bd90fca5ec85587de6f5e92f097720477f1648bd42d3983c27c17bea3df2eb66 3 Pass the details and signature to the widget Pass customerDetailsSignature next to customerDetails . The values in customerDetails must match the ones you signed. In this example, fetchCustomerFromBackend calls your own endpoint, which returns { customerDetails, customerDetailsSignature } for the signed-in customer, so the details and the signature come from one place. const { customerDetails , customerDetailsSignature } = await fetchCustomerFromBackend (); Plain . init ({ customerDetails , customerDetailsSignature , }); Fetch a new payload when the email or the identifiers change. Plain rejects the request when externalId or tenantIdentifier differs from what you signed. The signature is a bearer credential: anyone who has it can claim that customer. Verify an unknown email Set requireAuthentication when the customer isn’t signed in and you want an email before they can chat. This fits a public page where you don’t manage sign-in. You can pair this option with the Require email verification setting in Settings → Chat . Plain . init ({ requireAuthentication: true , }); The widget asks for an email address. Plain emails a one-time code. After the customer enters the code, they can start a thread . Was this page helpful?
